People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },

    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

Can you tell me what brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing that finally made me come in is my eating. It’s been really up and down. Some weeks I barely want dinner at all, and then other nights I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers and sweets. It just feels kind of out of control.\n\nDoctor: After times when you feel like you've eaten a lot or lost control, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I don’t make myself throw up or take laxatives. And I wouldn’t say I exercise to make up for it either. I usually just feel bad about it afterward and try to eat less the next day, but nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you lose control and eat a lot in one sitting, and have you noticed any pattern in when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s not every day, but it comes in spells. Some weeks I’m hardly eating much at dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s gone to bed, eating crackers or sweets and feeling like I’m not really stopping when I meant to. The pattern seems to be more at night, when it’s quiet and I’m by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating late at night happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or down, or do they seem to happen more randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel totally random, no. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets. I don’t always know what sets it off, but the late-night part is definitely when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes, do you notice that you're eating much more quickly than normal or feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like once I’m there eating, I don’t really stop when I meant to. I wouldn’t say I’m always eating super fast, but I’ll be standing there having crackers or sweets, and then it’s more than I planned. That’s part of what pushed me to come in—my eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the kitchen late at night eating, do you feel like you can't stop or control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least sometimes. I’ll go in thinking I’ll just have a little something, and then I’m standing there eating crackers or sweets after everyone’s gone to bed, and it ends up being more than I meant to. It’s part of why I came in—my eating just feels really all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of eating affected your daily life, like causing you distress, affecting your relationships, or making it hard to focus on work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. It’s been causing me stress, and I feel like I’ve fallen behind on things I’m supposed to be doing, like my facilitator training and work on my private blog. My projects and even explaining things have gotten harder to organize.\n\nAnd at home, there’s been more tension, so it’s harder to keep normal routines with my husband. It’s not completely taking over everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself or feel bad about your body shape or weight after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I focus much on my body shape or weight right after. I might feel annoyed with myself for eating when I didn’t plan to, but I haven’t really noticed it being about how I look.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I usually just feel bad about it afterward and try to eat less the next day, but nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you lose control and eat a lot in one sitting, and have you noticed any pattern in when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s not every day, but it comes in spells. The pattern seems to be more at night, when it’s quiet and I’m by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating late at night happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or down, or do they seem to happen more randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel totally random, no. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets. I don’t always know what sets it off, but the late-night part is definitely when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes, do you notice that you're eating much more quickly than normal or feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like once I’m there eating, I don’t really stop when I meant to. I wouldn’t say I’m always eating super fast, but I’ll be standing there having crackers or sweets, and then it’s more than I planned. That’s part of what pushed me to come in—my eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the kitchen late at night eating, do you feel like you can't stop or control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least sometimes. I’ll go in thinking I’ll just have a little something, and then I’m standing there eating crackers or sweets after everyone’s gone to bed, and it ends up being more than I meant to. It’s part of why I came in—my eating just feels really all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of eating affected your daily life, like causing you distress, affecting your relationships, or making it hard to focus on work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. It’s been causing me stress, and I feel like I’ve fallen behind on things I’m supposed to be doing, like my facilitator training and work on my private blog. I don’t always know what sets it off, but the late-night part is definitely when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes, do you notice that you're eating much more quickly than normal or feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like once I’m there eating, I don’t really stop when I meant to. It’s more uneven than that—some weeks I barely eat dinner, and then other nights I’ll end up in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets. It feels out of control in a frustrating way, but more like picking at things or snacking than one big binge in a couple hours.

It just feels kind of out of control.\n\nDoctor: After times when you feel like you've eaten a lot or lost control, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I usually just feel bad about it afterward and try to eat less the next day, but nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you lose control and eat a lot in one sitting, and have you noticed any pattern in when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s not every day, but it comes in spells. The pattern seems to be more at night, when it’s quiet and I’m by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating late at night happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or down, or do they seem to happen more randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel totally random, no. I don’t always know what sets it off, but the late-night part is definitely when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes, do you notice that you're eating much more quickly than normal or feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like once I’m there eating, I don’t really stop when I meant to. I wouldn’t say I’m always eating super fast, but I’ll be standing there having crackers or sweets, and then it’s more than I planned. Some weeks I barely want dinner at all, and then other nights I’m standing in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers and sweets. It just feels kind of out of control.\n\nDoctor: After times when you feel like you've eaten a lot or lost control, do you ever make yourself vomit, use laxatives, or exercise a lot to try to balance it out?\n\nPatient: No, I don’t do that. I don’t make myself throw up or take laxatives. And I wouldn’t say I exercise to make up for it either. I usually just feel bad about it afterward and try to eat less the next day, but nothing like that.\n\nDoctor: How often do you feel like you lose control and eat a lot in one sitting, and have you noticed any pattern in when these episodes happen?\n\nPatient: It’s not every day, but it comes in spells. The pattern seems to be more at night, when it’s quiet and I’m by myself.\n\nDoctor: Do these episodes of eating late at night happen when you're feeling stressed, anxious, or down, or do they seem to happen more randomly?\n\nPatient: It doesn’t feel totally random, no. It’s more like my eating is just all over the place. Some weeks I barely touch dinner, and then other nights, after everyone’s gone to bed, I’ll be standing in the kitchen eating crackers or sweets. I don’t always know what sets it off, but the late-night part is definitely when it tends to happen.\n\nDoctor: When you find yourself eating during these late-night episodes, do you notice that you're eating much more quickly than normal or feel like you can't stop once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah, sometimes it feels like once I’m there eating, I don’t really stop when I meant to. I wouldn’t say I’m always eating super fast, but I’ll be standing there having crackers or sweets, and then it’s more than I planned. That’s part of what pushed me to come in—my eating just feels all over the place.\n\nDoctor: When you're in the kitchen late at night eating, do you feel like you can't stop or control how much you eat once you start?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so, at least sometimes. I’ll go in thinking I’ll just have a little something, and then I’m standing there eating crackers or sweets after everyone’s gone to bed, and it ends up being more than I meant to. It’s part of why I came in—my eating just feels really all over the place.\n\nDoctor: Has this pattern of eating affected your daily life, like causing you distress, affecting your relationships, or making it hard to focus on work or other activities?\n\nPatient: Yes, it has. It’s been causing me stress, and I feel like I’ve fallen behind on things I’m supposed to be doing, like my facilitator training and work on my private blog. My projects and even explaining things have gotten harder to organize.\n\nAnd at home, there’s been more tension, so it’s harder to keep normal routines with my husband. It’s not completely taking over everything, but it is affecting my day-to-day life.\n\nDoctor: Do you judge yourself or feel bad about your body shape or weight after these eating episodes?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure. I don’t think I focus much on my body shape or weight right after. I might feel annoyed with myself for eating when I didn’t plan to, but I haven’t really noticed it being about how I look.\n\nDoctor: Do you feel a lot of distress or upset specifically about these late-night eating episodes, like feeling ashamed or really frustrated with yourself afterward?\n\nPatient: Yeah, I do get upset about it. I wouldn’t say it’s always shame exactly, but it’s frustrating. What really pushed me to come in is that my eating feels so all over the place—some weeks I barely eat dinner, and then other nights I’m in the kitchen after everyone’s asleep eating crackers or sweets. That’s part of what’s been bothering me—my eating is just all over the place.
